use super::Clock;

/// A timeout, represented in one of the two ways.
#[derive(Clone, Debug)]
pub enum Timeout {
    /// The timeout is reached _after_ the `Duration` time is elapsed.
    After(Duration),
    /// The timeout is reached _when_ the clock's time is equal to `Duration`.
    When(Duration),
}

/// A timer with periodic functionality.
///
/// Setting the timer will trigger a callback function upon expiration of
/// the set time. To enable its periodic functionality, users should set
/// its `interval` field with [`TimerGuard::set_interval`]. By doing this,
/// the timer will use the interval time to configure a new timing after expiration.
pub struct Timer {
    inner: SpinLock<TimerInner>,
    timer_manager: Arc<TimerManager>,
    registered_callback: Box<dyn Fn(TimerGuard) + Send + Sync>,
}

#[derive(Default)]
struct TimerInner {
    interval: Duration,
    timer_callback: Weak<TimerCallback>,
}

/// A guard that provides exclusive access to a `Timer`.
pub struct TimerGuard<'a> {
    inner: SpinLockGuard<'a, TimerInner, LocalIrqDisabled>,
    timer: &'a Arc<Timer>,
}

impl TimerGuard<'_> {
    /// Sets the interval time for this timer.
    ///
    /// The timer will be reset with the interval time upon expiration.
    pub fn set_interval(&mut self, interval: Duration) {
        self.inner.interval = interval;
    }

    /// Sets the timer with a timeout.
    ///
    /// The registered callback function of this timer will be invoked
    /// when reaching timeout. If the timer has a valid interval, this timer
    /// will be set again with the interval when reaching timeout.
    pub fn set_timeout(&mut self, timeout: Timeout) {
        let expired_time = match timeout {
            Timeout::After(timeout) => {
                let now = self.timer.timer_manager.clock.read_time();
                now + timeout
            }
            Timeout::When(timeout) => timeout,
        };

        let timer_weak = Arc::downgrade(self.timer);
        let new_timer_callback = Arc::new(TimerCallback::new(expired_time, timer_weak));

        if let Some(timer_callback) = self.inner.timer_callback.upgrade() {
            timer_callback.cancel();
        }

        self.inner.timer_callback = Arc::downgrade(&new_timer_callback);
        self.timer.timer_manager.insert(new_timer_callback);
    }

    /// Cancels the currently set `TimerCallback`.
    ///
    /// Once cancelled, the current `TimerCallback` will not be triggered again.
    pub fn cancel(&self) {
        if let Some(timer_callback) = self.inner.timer_callback.upgrade() {
            timer_callback.cancel();
        }
    }

    /// Returns the current expired time of this timer.
    pub fn expired_time(&self) -> Duration {
        let timer_callback = self.inner.timer_callback.upgrade();
        timer_callback
            .and_then(|callback| (!callback.is_cancelled()).then_some(callback.expired_time))
            .unwrap_or(Duration::ZERO)
    }

    /// Returns the remain time to expiration of this timer.
    ///
    /// If the timer has not been set, this method
    /// will return `Duration::ZERO`.
    pub fn remain(&self) -> Duration {
        let now = self.timer.timer_manager.clock.read_time();
        let expired_time = self.expired_time();
        if expired_time > now {
            expired_time - now
        } else {
            Duration::ZERO
        }
    }

    /// Returns the interval time of the current timer.
    pub fn interval(&self) -> Duration {
        self.inner.interval
    }
}

impl Timer {
    /// Creates a `Timer` instance from a [`TimerManager`].
    /// This timer will be managed by the `TimerManager`.
    ///
    /// Note that if the callback instructions involves sleep, users should put these instructions
    /// into something like `WorkQueue` to avoid sleeping during system timer interruptions.
    fn new<F>(registered_callback: F, timer_manager: Arc<TimerManager>) -> Arc<Self>
    where
        F: Fn(TimerGuard) + Send + Sync + 'static,
    {
        Arc::new(Self {
            inner: SpinLock::new(TimerInner::default()),
            timer_manager,
            registered_callback: Box::new(registered_callback),
        })
    }

    /// Locks the timer and returns a [`TimerGuard`] for exclusive access.
    pub fn lock(self: &Arc<Self>) -> TimerGuard<'_> {
        TimerGuard {
            inner: self.inner.disable_irq().lock(),
            timer: self,
        }
    }

    /// Returns a reference to the [`TimerManager`] which manages
    /// the current timer.
    pub fn timer_manager(&self) -> &Arc<TimerManager> {
        &self.timer_manager
    }
}

/// `TimerManager` is used to create timers and manage their expiries. It holds a clock and can
/// create [`Timer`]s based on this clock.
///
/// These created `Timer`s will hold an `Arc` pointer to this manager, hence this manager
/// will be actually dropped after all the created timers have been dropped.
pub struct TimerManager {
    clock: Arc<dyn Clock>,
    timer_callbacks: SpinLock<BinaryHeap<Arc<TimerCallback>>>,
}

impl TimerManager {
    /// Creates a `TimerManager` instance from a clock.
    pub fn new(clock: Arc<dyn Clock>) -> Arc<Self> {
        Arc::new(Self {
            clock,
            timer_callbacks: SpinLock::new(BinaryHeap::new()),
        })
    }

    /// Returns the clock associated with this timer manager.
    pub fn clock(&self) -> &Arc<dyn Clock> {
        &self.clock
    }

    /// Returns whether a given `timeout` is expired.
    pub fn is_expired_timeout(&self, timeout: &Timeout) -> bool {
        match timeout {
            Timeout::After(duration) => *duration == Duration::ZERO,

    /// (i.e., [`Timeout::After`]), this method will freeze the timeout by converting it to a fixed
    /// instant (i.e., [`Timeout::When`]).
    pub fn freeze(&mut self) {
        self.timeout = match self.timeout {
            Timeout::When(instant) => Timeout::When(instant),
            Timeout::After(duration) => Timeout::When(self.manager.clock().read_time() + duration),
        }
    }

    /// Creates a timer for the timeout.
    pub fn create_timer<F>(&self, callback: F) -> Arc<Timer>
    where
        F: Fn(TimerGuard) + Send + Sync + 'static,
    {
        let timer = self.manager.create_timer(callback);
        timer.lock().set_timeout(self.timeout.clone());
        timer
    }
}

 succeeded in 0ms:
            Timeout::After(duration) => *duration == Duration::ZERO,
            Timeout::When(duration) => {
                let now = self.clock.read_time();
                now >= *duration
            }
        }
    }

    fn insert(&self, timer_callback: Arc<TimerCallback>) {
        self.timer_callbacks
            .disable_irq()
            .lock()
            .push(timer_callback);
    }

    /// Checks and processes the managed timers.
    ///
    /// If any of the timers have timed out, call the corresponding callback functions.
    pub fn process_expired_timers(&self) {
        let callbacks = {
            let mut timeout_list = self.timer_callbacks.disable_irq().lock();
            if timeout_list.is_empty() {
                return;
            }

            let mut callbacks = Vec::new();
            let current_time = self.clock.read_time();
            while let Some(t) = timeout_list.peek() {
                if t.is_cancelled() {
                    // Just ignore the cancelled callback
                    timeout_list.pop();
                } else if t.expired_time <= current_time {
                    callbacks.push(timeout_list.pop().unwrap());
                } else {
                    break;
                }
            }
            callbacks
        };

        for callback in callbacks {
            callback.call();
        }
    }

    /// Creates an [`Timer`], which will be managed by this `TimerManager`.
    pub fn create_timer<F>(self: &Arc<Self>, function: F) -> Arc<Timer>
    where
        F: Fn(TimerGuard) + Send + Sync + 'static,
    {
        Timer::new(function, self.clone())
    }
}

/// A `TimerCallback` can be used to execute a timer callback function.
struct TimerCallback {
    expired_time: Duration,
    timer: Weak<Timer>,
    is_cancelled: AtomicBool,
}

impl TimerCallback {
    /// Creates an instance of `TimerCallback`.
    fn new(timeout: Duration, timer: Weak<Timer>) -> Self {
        Self {
            expired_time: timeout,
            timer,
            is_cancelled: AtomicBool::new(false),
        }
    }

    /// Cancels a `TimerCallback`. If the callback function has not been called,
    /// it will never be called again.
    fn cancel(&self) {
        self.is_cancelled.store(true, Ordering::Release);
    }

    // Returns whether the `TimerCallback` is cancelled.
    fn is_cancelled(&self) -> bool {
        self.is_cancelled.load(Ordering::Acquire)
    }

    fn call(&self) {
        let Some(timer) = self.timer.upgrade() else {
            return;
        };

        let mut timer_guard = timer.lock();

        if self.is_cancelled() {
            // The callback is cancelled.
            return;
        }

        let interval = timer_guard.interval();
        if interval != Duration::ZERO {
            timer_guard.set_timeout(Timeout::After(interval));
        }

        // Pass the `timer_guard` guard to the callback, allowing it to prevent race conditions.
        // The callback may choose to use the guard or drop it if not needed.
        (timer.registered_callback)(timer_guard);
    }
}

impl PartialEq for TimerCallback {
    fn eq(&self, other: &Self) -> bool {
        self.expired_time == other.expired_time
    }
}

impl Eq for TimerCallback {}

impl PartialOrd for TimerCallback {
    fn partial_cmp(&self, other: &Self) -> Option<core::cmp::Ordering> {
        Some(self.cmp(other))
    }
}

impl Ord for TimerCallback {
    fn cmp(&self, other: &Self) -> core::cmp::Ordering {
        // We want `TimerCallback`s to be processed in ascending order of `expired_time`,
        // and the in-order management of `TimerCallback`s currently relies on a maximum heap,
        // so we need the reverse instruction here.
        self.expired_time.cmp(&other.expired_time).reverse()
    }
}

 succeeded in 0ms:
        if let Some(timer) = timer
            && !res
                .as_ref()
                .is_err_and(|e: &Error| e.error() == Errno::ETIME)
        {
            timer.lock().cancel();
        }

        res
    }
}

impl WaitTimeout for WaitQueue {
    fn wait_until_or_timeout_cancelled<F, R, FCancel>(
        &self,
        mut cond: F,
        cancel_cond: FCancel,
        timeout: Option<&ManagedTimeout>,
    ) -> Result<R>
    where
        F: FnMut() -> Option<R>,
        FCancel: Fn() -> Result<()>,
    {
        // Fast path:
        if let Some(res) = cond() {
            return Ok(res);
        }

        let (waiter, _) = Waiter::new_pair();
        let cond = || {
            self.enqueue(waiter.waker());
            cond()
        };
        waiter.wait_until_or_timeout_cancelled(cond, cancel_cond, timeout)
    }
}

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0

use ostd::mm::VmIo;

use super::SyscallReturn;
use crate::{
    ipc::{
        IpcControlCmd, IpcId,
        semaphore::system_v::{PermissionMode, sem::Semaphore},
    },
    prelude::*,
    process::Pid,
};

pub fn sys_semctl(
    semid: i32,
    semnum: i32,
    op: i32,
    arg: Vaddr,
    ctx: &Context,
) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    let Ok(semid) = IpcId::try_from(semid.cast_unsigned()) else {
        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "non-positive semaphore IDs are invalid");
    };
    let cmd = IpcControlCmd::try_from(op)?;

    debug!(
        "semctl: semid = {:?}, semnum = {}, cmd = {:?}, arg = {:x}",
        semid, semnum, cmd, arg
    );

    let ns_proxy = ctx.thread_local.borrow_ns_proxy();
    let ipc_ns = ns_proxy.unwrap().ipc_ns();

    match cmd {
        IpcControlCmd::IPC_RMID => {
            let euid = ctx.posix_thread.credentials().euid();
            ipc_ns.remove_sem_set(semid, |sem_set| {
                // TODO: Consider capabilities in addition to UIDs.
                let permission = sem_set.permission();
                let can_remove = (euid == permission.uid()) || (euid == permission.cuid());
                if !can_remove {
                    return_errno_with_message!(
                        Errno::EPERM,
                        "the process does not have permission to remove the semaphore set"
                    );
                }

                Ok(())
            })?;
        }
        IpcControlCmd::IPC_STAT => {
            ipc_ns.with_sem_set(semid, PermissionMode::READ, |sem_set| {
                let semid_ds = sem_set.semid_ds();
                Ok(ctx.user_space().write_val(arg as Vaddr, &semid_ds)?)
            })?;
        }
        IpcControlCmd::SEM_GETPID => {
            fn sem_pid(sem: &Semaphore) -> Pid {
                sem.latest_modified_pid()
            }
            let pid: Pid = ipc_ns.with_sem_set(semid, PermissionMode::READ, |sem_set| {
                sem_set.get(semnum as usize, sem_pid)
            })?;

            return Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(pid as isize));
        }
        IpcControlCmd::SEM_GETVAL => {
            fn sem_val(sem: &Semaphore) -> i32 {
                sem.val()
            }
            let val: i32 = ipc_ns.with_sem_set(semid, PermissionMode::READ, |sem_set| {
                sem_set.get(semnum as usize, sem_val)
            })?;

            return Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(val as isize));
        }
        IpcControlCmd::SEM_GETNCNT => {
            let cnt: usize = ipc_ns.with_sem_set(semid, PermissionMode::READ, |sem_set| {
                sem_set.pending_alter_count(semnum as usize)
            })?;

            return Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(cnt as isize));
        }
        IpcControlCmd::SEM_GETZCNT => {
            let cnt: usize = ipc_ns.with_sem_set(semid, PermissionMode::READ, |sem_set| {
                sem_set.pending_const_count(semnum as usize)
            })?;

            return Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(cnt as isize));
        }
        IpcControlCmd::SEM_SETVAL => {
            // In `SEM_SETVAL`, the argument is parsed as an `i32`.
            let val = arg as i32;

            ipc_ns.with_sem_set(semid, PermissionMode::ALTER, |sem_set| {
                sem_set.setval(semnum as usize, val, ctx.process.pid())
            })?;
        }
        _ => todo!("Need to support {:?} in SYS_SEMCTL", cmd),
    }

    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
}

# Summary

<!-- SUMMARY -->

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 42

> ```diff
> 40	#[repr(u16)]
> 41	#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, TryFromInt)]
> 42	pub(super) enum Status {
> 43	    Normal = 0,
> 44	    Pending = 1,
> 45	    Removed = 2,
> ```

`descriptive-names` (minor): `Status` is imported into `sem_set.rs` and used as `Status::Removed` and `Status::Normal`, but the name does not say it is the lifecycle state of a `PendingOp`; `Normal` is especially unclear because it means the pending operation completed normally.

**Fix.** Rename `Status` to `PendingOpStatus` and rename `Normal` to `Completed` or another state name that describes the completed outcome. Update `AtomicStatus` to match the new type name.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 158

> ```diff
> 158	    let mut timer = None;
> ...
> 193	            jiffies_timer.lock().set_timeout(Timeout::After(timeout));
> 194	            timer = Some(jiffies_timer);
> ```

`explain-why` (minor): `timer` is never read after assignment; it is kept only so the `Arc<Timer>` outlives `waiter.wait()`. That lifetime dependency is non-obvious because the timer callback stores only a `Weak<Timer>`, so a reader can easily remove or shorten this binding while cleaning up apparent dead state.

**Fix.** Rename `timer` to something like `timeout_timer` and add a short comment explaining that the handle must stay alive until after `waiter.wait()` returns.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 167

> ```diff
> 166	                for wake_op in wake_queue {
> 167	                    wake_op.set_status(Status::Normal);
> 168	                    if let Some(waker) = wake_op.waker {
> 169	                        waker.wake_up();
> 170	                    }
> 171	                }
> ```

`dry` (minor): The `set_status` plus optional `waker.wake_up()` sequence is repeated here and in `sem_set.rs` for `setval` and `Drop`. This duplicates the rule for finishing a `PendingOp`, and this copy already bypasses the `waker()` accessor by reading `wake_op.waker` directly.

**Fix.** Move the sequence into a `PendingOp` helper such as `wake_with_status(&self, status: Status)` and call it from all wake paths.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 315

> ```diff
> 314	/// Iter the sops and return the flags (alter, dupsop)
> 315	fn get_sops_flags(pending_op: &PendingOp) -> (bool, bool) {
> 316	    let mut alter = false;
> 317	    let mut dupsop = false;
> ```

`bool-names` (minor): `get_sops_flags()` returns an unlabeled `(bool, bool)` and names the values `alter` and `dupsop`. At the call site, `let (alter, dupsop)` does not read as positive assertions and `dupsop` is an opaque abbreviation.

**Fix.** Return a small struct with assertion-style field names, for example `SopsFlags { has_alter_op, has_duplicate_alter_op }`, and use those names at the call site.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 341

> ```diff
> 341	fn perform_atomic_semop(sems: &mut [Semaphore], pending_op: &mut PendingOp) -> Result<bool> {
> 342	    for op in pending_op.sops_iter() {
> ...
> 381	            sem.latest_modified_pid = pending_op.pid;
> ```

`least-surprise` (minor): `perform_atomic_semop()` takes `pending_op` as `&mut PendingOp`, but it only reads `pending_op.sops_iter()` and `pending_op.pid`. The mutable parameter suggests the operation bookkeeping may be changed, which makes callers harder to reason about.

**Fix.** Change the parameter to `pending_op: &PendingOp` and pass immutable references from the callers.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 212

> ```diff
>             waiter.wait();
>             match status.load(Ordering::Relaxed) {
>                 Status::Normal => Ok(()),
>                 Status::Removed => Err(Error::new(Errno::EIDRM)),
>                 Status::Pending => {
>                     // FIXME: Lookup may be time-consuming.
>                     ipc_ns.with_sem_set(sem_id, PermissionMode::empty(), |sem_set| {
>                         let mut inner = sem_set.inner();
>                         let pending_ops = if alter {
>                             &mut inner.pending_alter
>                         } else {
>                             &mut inner.pending_const
>                         };
>                         pending_ops.retain(|op| !Arc::ptr_eq(&op.status, &status));
> ```

`atomic-critical-sections` (major): The timeout path checks `status` before taking the semaphore-set lock, then removes the queued operation later. A concrete race is: the timer wakes the waiter, this load sees `Status::Pending`, another thread completes the queued `semop` and sets `Status::Normal`, then this thread reaches `retain()` and returns `EAGAIN` even though its operation already modified the semaphore.

**Fix.** Move the `Status::Pending` check into the same critical section as queue removal, and decide the return value from the status observed while holding the semaphore-set lock. Only return `EAGAIN` if the operation is still queued and is actually removed for timeout.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 343

> ```diff
>     for op in pending_op.sops_iter() {
>         let flags = IpcFlags::from_bits_truncate(op.sem_flags as u32);
> 
>         let Some(sem) = sems.get(op.sem_num as usize) else {
> ```

Incorrect validation (major): `IpcFlags::from_bits_truncate()` silently drops invalid user-supplied `sem_flags` bits. For example, a `semop` with `sem_flags = 0x4000` is accepted and applied instead of failing with `EINVAL` for unsupported flags.

**Fix.** Validate `sem_flags` with `IpcFlags::from_bits()` and return `EINVAL` when any unknown bit is present, before using the flags.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 348

> ```diff
>         let Some(sem) = sems.get(op.sem_num as usize) else {
>             return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EFBIG, "the semaphore number is out of bounds");
>         };
>         let mut result = sem.val();
> 
>         // Zero condition
>         if op.sem_op == 0 && result != 0 {
> ```

Incorrect multi-operation accounting (major): Each validation pass starts from the original `sem.val()` instead of the value after earlier operations in the same `semop` array. With initial value `1` and two operations `{ sem_num: 0, sem_op: -1 }`, both checks pass against `1`, then the commit pass applies both and leaves `sem.val` at `-1`; conversely `{ +1, -1 }` on value `0` incorrectly blocks.

**Fix.** Validate against scratch semaphore values that are updated in array order, then commit those checked values to `sems` only after the whole array succeeds.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 372

> ```diff
>         if flags.contains(IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO) {
>             todo!()
>         }
> ```

Reachable panic (critical): A userspace `semop` with `SEM_UNDO` reaches `todo!()` from the syscall path. For example, `{ sem_num: 0, sem_op: 1, sem_flg: SEM_UNDO }` passes the preceding value checks and panics the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or implementing undo accounting.

**Fix.** Implement `SEM_UNDO` accounting, or reject `SEM_UNDO` with a normal error until it is supported; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from `sys_semop` or `sys_semtimedop`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 160

> ```diff
> 145    let (alter, dupsop) = get_sops_flags(&pending_op);
> ...
> 160    let sem_op_result = ipc_ns.with_sem_set(sem_id, PermissionMode::empty(), |sem_set| {
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `sem_op()` accepts a user-selected `sem_id` and operation vector, but calls `ipc_ns.with_sem_set()` with `PermissionMode::empty()`. A process that can name another user's semaphore set can submit a nonzero `sem_op` and alter it without the required `PermissionMode::ALTER`; zero-wait operations also bypass the required `PermissionMode::READ`.

**Fix.** Derive the required permission from `sops` before entering the set and pass it to `IpcNamespace::with_sem_set()`, for example `PermissionMode::ALTER` when any `sem_op` is nonzero and `PermissionMode::READ` otherwise. The namespace permission check must reject unauthorized callers instead of only warning.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 371

> ```diff
> 343        let flags = IpcFlags::from_bits_truncate(op.sem_flags as u32);
> ...
> 371        if flags.contains(IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO) {
> 372            todo!()
> 373        }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `sem_flags` comes from user memory, but `perform_atomic_semop()` truncates it and then reaches `todo!()` when `IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO` is present. An unprivileged caller can pass `SEM_UNDO` on an otherwise valid operation, or pass `-1` so truncation includes `SEM_UNDO`, and panic the kernel.

**Fix.** Validate `sem_flags` against a semop-specific mask before executing the operation. Until `SEM_UNDO` is implemented, return an error for `IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO` instead of calling `todo!()`.

## Documentation

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 194

> ```diff
> +            jiffies_timer.lock().set_timeout(Timeout::After(timeout));
> +            timer = Some(jiffies_timer);
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): This change makes the `semtimedop` timeout path work by keeping the timer alive, which enhances user-visible syscall behavior. The Linux Compatibility docs are not updated in the same change: `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/syscall-flag-coverage/inter-process-communication/semop_and_semtimedop.scml` still only shows nonblocking `semop` with `IPC_NOWAIT`, and the page says `semtimedop` coverage is the same as `semop`.

**Fix.** Update the `semop`/`semtimedop` Linux Compatibility page and its `.scml` coverage file to document the now-supported timed blocking `semtimedop` behavior, including the `timeout` argument behavior and any remaining unsupported cases.

# Summary

The reviewed file implements System V semaphore operations and the recent working-tree change fixes timeout lifetime and waiter-retain behavior. The direction is good, and the new timeout handle is necessary for the timer callback to remain reachable.

Top issues:

- Critical: `SEM_UNDO` is still reachable from userspace and panics through `todo!()`.
- Critical: `semop` enters the semaphore set with `PermissionMode::empty()`, so the operation vector does not drive read/alter permission enforcement.
- Major: a timeout wake can race with a real completion because `Status::Pending` is checked outside the queue-removal critical section.
- Major: multi-operation `semop` validation does not account for earlier operations in the same operation array.
- Major: the `semtimedop` behavior improvement needs a matching Linux compatibility coverage update.

The maintainability comments are lower severity but point at real future hazards: the timer lifetime is easy to mistake for dead state, and the pending-operation wake/status rule is duplicated across wake paths.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 42

> ```diff
> 40	#[repr(u16)]
> 41	#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, TryFromInt)]
> 42	pub(super) enum Status {
> 43	    Normal = 0,
> 44	    Pending = 1,
> 45	    Removed = 2,
> ```

`descriptive-names` (minor): `Status` is imported into `sem_set.rs` and used as `Status::Removed` and `Status::Normal`, but the name does not say it is the lifecycle state of a `PendingOp`; `Normal` is especially unclear because it means the pending operation completed normally.

**Fix.** Rename `Status` to `PendingOpStatus` and rename `Normal` to `Completed` or another state name that describes the completed outcome. Update `AtomicStatus` to match the new type name.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 158

> ```diff
> 158	    let mut timer = None;
> ...
> 193	            jiffies_timer.lock().set_timeout(Timeout::After(timeout));
> 194	            timer = Some(jiffies_timer);
> ```

`explain-why` (minor): `timer` is never read after assignment; it is kept only so the `Arc<Timer>` outlives `waiter.wait()`. That lifetime dependency is non-obvious because the timer callback stores only a `Weak<Timer>`, so a reader can easily remove or shorten this binding while cleaning up apparent dead state.

**Fix.** Rename `timer` to something like `timeout_timer` and add a short comment explaining that the handle must stay alive until after `waiter.wait()` returns.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 167

> ```diff
> 166	                for wake_op in wake_queue {
> 167	                    wake_op.set_status(Status::Normal);
> 168	                    if let Some(waker) = wake_op.waker {
> 169	                        waker.wake_up();
> 170	                    }
> 171	                }
> ```

`dry` (minor): The `set_status` plus optional `waker.wake_up()` sequence is repeated here and in `sem_set.rs` for `setval` and `Drop`. This duplicates the rule for finishing a `PendingOp`, and this copy already bypasses the `waker()` accessor by reading `wake_op.waker` directly.

**Fix.** Move the sequence into a `PendingOp` helper such as `wake_with_status(&self, status: Status)` and call it from all wake paths.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 315

> ```diff
> 314	/// Iter the sops and return the flags (alter, dupsop)
> 315	fn get_sops_flags(pending_op: &PendingOp) -> (bool, bool) {
> 316	    let mut alter = false;
> 317	    let mut dupsop = false;
> ```

`bool-names` (minor): `get_sops_flags()` returns an unlabeled `(bool, bool)` and names the values `alter` and `dupsop`. At the call site, `let (alter, dupsop)` does not read as positive assertions and `dupsop` is an opaque abbreviation.

**Fix.** Return a small struct with assertion-style field names, for example `SopsFlags { has_alter_op, has_duplicate_alter_op }`, and use those names at the call site.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 341

> ```diff
> 341	fn perform_atomic_semop(sems: &mut [Semaphore], pending_op: &mut PendingOp) -> Result<bool> {
> 342	    for op in pending_op.sops_iter() {
> ...
> 381	            sem.latest_modified_pid = pending_op.pid;
> ```

`least-surprise` (minor): `perform_atomic_semop()` takes `pending_op` as `&mut PendingOp`, but it only reads `pending_op.sops_iter()` and `pending_op.pid`. The mutable parameter suggests the operation bookkeeping may be changed, which makes callers harder to reason about.

**Fix.** Change the parameter to `pending_op: &PendingOp` and pass immutable references from the callers.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 212

> ```diff
>             waiter.wait();
>             match status.load(Ordering::Relaxed) {
>                 Status::Normal => Ok(()),
>                 Status::Removed => Err(Error::new(Errno::EIDRM)),
>                 Status::Pending => {
>                     // FIXME: Lookup may be time-consuming.
>                     ipc_ns.with_sem_set(sem_id, PermissionMode::empty(), |sem_set| {
>                         let mut inner = sem_set.inner();
>                         let pending_ops = if alter {
>                             &mut inner.pending_alter
>                         } else {
>                             &mut inner.pending_const
>                         };
>                         pending_ops.retain(|op| !Arc::ptr_eq(&op.status, &status));
> ```

`atomic-critical-sections` (major): The timeout path checks `status` before taking the semaphore-set lock, then removes the queued operation later. A concrete race is: the timer wakes the waiter, this load sees `Status::Pending`, another thread completes the queued `semop` and sets `Status::Normal`, then this thread reaches `retain()` and returns `EAGAIN` even though its operation already modified the semaphore.

**Fix.** Move the `Status::Pending` check into the same critical section as queue removal, and decide the return value from the status observed while holding the semaphore-set lock. Only return `EAGAIN` if the operation is still queued and is actually removed for timeout.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 348

> ```diff
>         let Some(sem) = sems.get(op.sem_num as usize) else {
>             return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EFBIG, "the semaphore number is out of bounds");
>         };
>         let mut result = sem.val();
> 
>         // Zero condition
>         if op.sem_op == 0 && result != 0 {
> ```

Incorrect multi-operation accounting (major): Each validation pass starts from the original `sem.val()` instead of the value after earlier operations in the same `semop` array. With initial value `1` and two operations `{ sem_num: 0, sem_op: -1 }`, both checks pass against `1`, then the commit pass applies both and leaves `sem.val` at `-1`; conversely `{ +1, -1 }` on value `0` incorrectly blocks.

**Fix.** Validate against scratch semaphore values that are updated in array order, then commit those checked values to `sems` only after the whole array succeeds.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 372

> ```diff
>         if flags.contains(IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO) {
>             todo!()
>         }
> ```

Reachable panic (critical): A userspace `semop` with `SEM_UNDO` reaches `todo!()` from the syscall path. For example, `{ sem_num: 0, sem_op: 1, sem_flg: SEM_UNDO }` passes the preceding value checks and panics the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or implementing undo accounting.

**Fix.** Shared with the Security `SEM_UNDO` comment: implement `SEM_UNDO` accounting, or reject `SEM_UNDO` with a normal error until it is supported; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from `sys_semop` or `sys_semtimedop`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 160

> ```diff
> 145    let (alter, dupsop) = get_sops_flags(&pending_op);
> ...
> 160    let sem_op_result = ipc_ns.with_sem_set(sem_id, PermissionMode::empty(), |sem_set| {
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `sem_op()` accepts a user-selected `sem_id` and operation vector, but calls `ipc_ns.with_sem_set()` with `PermissionMode::empty()`. A process that can name another user's semaphore set can submit a nonzero `sem_op` and alter it without the required `PermissionMode::ALTER`; zero-wait operations also bypass the required `PermissionMode::READ`.

**Fix.** Derive the required permission from `sops` before entering the set and pass it to `IpcNamespace::with_sem_set()`, for example `PermissionMode::ALTER` when any `sem_op` is nonzero and `PermissionMode::READ` otherwise. The namespace permission check must reject unauthorized callers instead of only warning.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 371

> ```diff
> 343        let flags = IpcFlags::from_bits_truncate(op.sem_flags as u32);
> ...
> 371        if flags.contains(IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO) {
> 372            todo!()
> 373        }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `sem_flags` comes from user memory, but `perform_atomic_semop()` truncates it and then reaches `todo!()` when `IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO` is present. An unprivileged caller can pass `SEM_UNDO` on an otherwise valid operation, or pass `-1` so truncation includes `SEM_UNDO`, and panic the kernel.

**Fix.** Shared with the Correctness `SEM_UNDO` comment: implement `SEM_UNDO` accounting, or reject `SEM_UNDO` with a normal error until it is supported; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from `sys_semop` or `sys_semtimedop`.

## Documentation

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 194

> ```diff
> +            jiffies_timer.lock().set_timeout(Timeout::After(timeout));
> +            timer = Some(jiffies_timer);
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): This change makes the `semtimedop` timeout path work by keeping the timer alive, which enhances user-visible syscall behavior. The Linux Compatibility docs are not updated in the same change: `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/syscall-flag-coverage/inter-process-communication/semop_and_semtimedop.scml` still only shows nonblocking `semop` with `IPC_NOWAIT`, and the page says `semtimedop` coverage is the same as `semop`.

**Fix.** Update the `semop`/`semtimedop` Linux Compatibility page and its `.scml` coverage file to document the now-supported timed blocking `semtimedop` behavior, including the `timeout` argument behavior and any remaining unsupported cases.

## Retracted by verification

- `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 343: retracted the invalid-unknown-`sem_flags` finding because Linux semop semantics define `IPC_NOWAIT` and `SEM_UNDO` as the meaningful `sem_flg` bits; the real defect here is the separately reported reachable `SEM_UNDO` panic, not accepting unrelated high bits.

# Expected defects

   defect: After the waiter wakes, `sem_op` reloads the operation's status and then acts on it in steps that are not atomic with respect to a concurrent removal or wakeup: the status it observed can change before it is acted upon, so an operation that should have completed is reported as a spurious `EAGAIN` (or the pending entry is mishandled).

# Summary

The reviewed file implements System V semaphore operations and the recent working-tree change fixes timeout lifetime and waiter-retain behavior. The direction is good, and the new timeout handle is necessary for the timer callback to remain reachable.

Top issues:

- Critical: `SEM_UNDO` is still reachable from userspace and panics through `todo!()`.
- Critical: `semop` enters the semaphore set with `PermissionMode::empty()`, so the operation vector does not drive read/alter permission enforcement.
- Major: a timeout wake can race with a real completion because `Status::Pending` is checked outside the queue-removal critical section.
- Major: multi-operation `semop` validation does not account for earlier operations in the same operation array.
- Major: the `semtimedop` behavior improvement needs a matching Linux compatibility coverage update.

The maintainability comments are lower severity but point at real future hazards: the timer lifetime is easy to mistake for dead state, and the pending-operation wake/status rule is duplicated across wake paths.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 42

> ```diff
> 40	#[repr(u16)]
> 41	#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, TryFromInt)]
> 42	pub(super) enum Status {
> 43	    Normal = 0,
> 44	    Pending = 1,
> 45	    Removed = 2,
> ```

`descriptive-names` (minor): `Status` is imported into `sem_set.rs` and used as `Status::Removed` and `Status::Normal`, but the name does not say it is the lifecycle state of a `PendingOp`; `Normal` is especially unclear because it means the pending operation completed normally.

**Fix.** Rename `Status` to `PendingOpStatus` and rename `Normal` to `Completed` or another state name that describes the completed outcome. Update `AtomicStatus` to match the new type name.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 158

> ```diff
> 158	    let mut timer = None;
> ...
> 193	            jiffies_timer.lock().set_timeout(Timeout::After(timeout));
> 194	            timer = Some(jiffies_timer);
> ```

`explain-why` (minor): `timer` is never read after assignment; it is kept only so the `Arc<Timer>` outlives `waiter.wait()`. That lifetime dependency is non-obvious because the timer callback stores only a `Weak<Timer>`, so a reader can easily remove or shorten this binding while cleaning up apparent dead state.

**Fix.** Rename `timer` to something like `timeout_timer` and add a short comment explaining that the handle must stay alive until after `waiter.wait()` returns.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 167

> ```diff
> 166	                for wake_op in wake_queue {
> 167	                    wake_op.set_status(Status::Normal);
> 168	                    if let Some(waker) = wake_op.waker {
> 169	                        waker.wake_up();
> 170	                    }
> 171	                }
> ```

`dry` (minor): The `set_status` plus optional `waker.wake_up()` sequence is repeated here and in `sem_set.rs` for `setval` and `Drop`. This duplicates the rule for finishing a `PendingOp`, and this copy already bypasses the `waker()` accessor by reading `wake_op.waker` directly.

**Fix.** Move the sequence into a `PendingOp` helper such as `wake_with_status({prompt}self, status: Status)` and call it from all wake paths.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 315

> ```diff
> 314	/// Iter the sops and return the flags (alter, dupsop)
> 315	fn get_sops_flags(pending_op: {prompt}PendingOp) -> (bool, bool) {
> 316	    let mut alter = false;
> 317	    let mut dupsop = false;
> ```

`bool-names` (minor): `get_sops_flags()` returns an unlabeled `(bool, bool)` and names the values `alter` and `dupsop`. At the call site, `let (alter, dupsop)` does not read as positive assertions and `dupsop` is an opaque abbreviation.

**Fix.** Return a small struct with assertion-style field names, for example `SopsFlags { has_alter_op, has_duplicate_alter_op }`, and use those names at the call site.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 341

> ```diff
> 341	fn perform_atomic_semop(sems: {prompt}mut [Semaphore], pending_op: {prompt}mut PendingOp) -> Result<bool> {
> 342	    for op in pending_op.sops_iter() {
> ...
> 381	            sem.latest_modified_pid = pending_op.pid;
> ```

`least-surprise` (minor): `perform_atomic_semop()` takes `pending_op` as `{prompt}mut PendingOp`, but it only reads `pending_op.sops_iter()` and `pending_op.pid`. The mutable parameter suggests the operation bookkeeping may be changed, which makes callers harder to reason about.

**Fix.** Change the parameter to `pending_op: {prompt}PendingOp` and pass immutable references from the callers.

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 212

> ```diff
>             waiter.wait();
>             match status.load(Ordering::Relaxed) {
>                 Status::Normal => Ok(()),
>                 Status::Removed => Err(Error::new(Errno::EIDRM)),
>                 Status::Pending => {
>                     // FIXME: Lookup may be time-consuming.
>                     ipc_ns.with_sem_set(sem_id, PermissionMode::empty(), |sem_set| {
>                         let mut inner = sem_set.inner();
>                         let pending_ops = if alter {
>                             {prompt}mut inner.pending_alter
>                         } else {
>                             {prompt}mut inner.pending_const
>                         };
>                         pending_ops.retain(|op| !Arc::ptr_eq({prompt}op.status, {prompt}status));
> ```

`atomic-critical-sections` (major): The timeout path checks `status` before taking the semaphore-set lock, then removes the queued operation later. A concrete race is: the timer wakes the waiter, this load sees `Status::Pending`, another thread completes the queued `semop` and sets `Status::Normal`, then this thread reaches `retain()` and returns `EAGAIN` even though its operation already modified the semaphore.

**Fix.** Move the `Status::Pending` check into the same critical section as queue removal, and decide the return value from the status observed while holding the semaphore-set lock. Only return `EAGAIN` if the operation is still queued and is actually removed for timeout.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 348

> ```diff
>         let Some(sem) = sems.get(op.sem_num as usize) else {
>             return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EFBIG, "the semaphore number is out of bounds");
>         };
>         let mut result = sem.val();
> 
>         // Zero condition
>         if op.sem_op == 0 {prompt}{prompt} result != 0 {
> ```

Incorrect multi-operation accounting (major): Each validation pass starts from the original `sem.val()` instead of the value after earlier operations in the same `semop` array. With initial value `1` and two operations `{ sem_num: 0, sem_op: -1 }`, both checks pass against `1`, then the commit pass applies both and leaves `sem.val` at `-1`; conversely `{ +1, -1 }` on value `0` incorrectly blocks.

**Fix.** Validate against scratch semaphore values that are updated in array order, then commit those checked values to `sems` only after the whole array succeeds.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 372

> ```diff
>         if flags.contains(IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO) {
>             todo!()
>         }
> ```

Reachable panic (critical): A userspace `semop` with `SEM_UNDO` reaches `todo!()` from the syscall path. For example, `{ sem_num: 0, sem_op: 1, sem_flg: SEM_UNDO }` passes the preceding value checks and panics the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or implementing undo accounting.

**Fix.** Shared with the Security `SEM_UNDO` comment: implement `SEM_UNDO` accounting, or reject `SEM_UNDO` with a normal error until it is supported; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from `sys_semop` or `sys_semtimedop`.

## Security

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 160

> ```diff
> 145    let (alter, dupsop) = get_sops_flags({prompt}pending_op);
> ...
> 160    let sem_op_result = ipc_ns.with_sem_set(sem_id, PermissionMode::empty(), |sem_set| {
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `sem_op()` accepts a user-selected `sem_id` and operation vector, but calls `ipc_ns.with_sem_set()` with `PermissionMode::empty()`. A process that can name another user's semaphore set can submit a nonzero `sem_op` and alter it without the required `PermissionMode::ALTER`; zero-wait operations also bypass the required `PermissionMode::READ`.

**Fix.** Derive the required permission from `sops` before entering the set and pass it to `IpcNamespace::with_sem_set()`, for example `PermissionMode::ALTER` when any `sem_op` is nonzero and `PermissionMode::READ` otherwise. The namespace permission check must reject unauthorized callers instead of only warning.

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 371

> ```diff
> 343        let flags = IpcFlags::from_bits_truncate(op.sem_flags as u32);
> ...
> 371        if flags.contains(IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO) {
> 372            todo!()
> 373        }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `sem_flags` comes from user memory, but `perform_atomic_semop()` truncates it and then reaches `todo!()` when `IpcFlags::SEM_UNDO` is present. An unprivileged caller can pass `SEM_UNDO` on an otherwise valid operation, or pass `-1` so truncation includes `SEM_UNDO`, and panic the kernel.

**Fix.** Shared with the Correctness `SEM_UNDO` comment: implement `SEM_UNDO` accounting, or reject `SEM_UNDO` with a normal error until it is supported; do not leave `todo!()` reachable from `sys_semop` or `sys_semtimedop`.

## Documentation

### `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 194

> ```diff
> +            jiffies_timer.lock().set_timeout(Timeout::After(timeout));
> +            timer = Some(jiffies_timer);
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): This change makes the `semtimedop` timeout path work by keeping the timer alive, which enhances user-visible syscall behavior. The Linux Compatibility docs are not updated in the same change: `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/syscall-flag-coverage/inter-process-communication/semop_and_semtimedop.scml` still only shows nonblocking `semop` with `IPC_NOWAIT`, and the page says `semtimedop` coverage is the same as `semop`.

**Fix.** Update the `semop`/`semtimedop` Linux Compatibility page and its `.scml` coverage file to document the now-supported timed blocking `semtimedop` behavior, including the `timeout` argument behavior and any remaining unsupported cases.

## Retracted by verification

- `kernel/src/ipc/semaphore/system_v/sem.rs` line 343: retracted the invalid-unknown-`sem_flags` finding because Linux semop semantics define `IPC_NOWAIT` and `SEM_UNDO` as the meaningful `sem_flg` bits; the real defect here is the separately reported reachable `SEM_UNDO` panic, not accepting unrelated high bits.
